> - **The shippable MSI MUST be built locally.** CI `touch`es 0-byte placeholder model fixtures (for tauri-build's compile-time resource validation), so a CI-built MSI would ship dead files and die at the integrity gate. Documented constraint, not a defect.
> - **Two build-environment lessons (each cost a failed build, both banked to [[feedback_cargo_on_windows_use_powershell]]):** (1) a fresh/cleaned release build needs VS2019 BuildTools vcvars activated or `openssl-src` (vendored OpenSSL, ADR-006) fails ~crate 527 — cc-rs auto-detects `cl` but openssl's own `nmake` build needs full vcvars on PATH; (2) the two heavy C++ CMake crates (llama.cpp + DuckDB) OOM a 16 GB machine when built concurrently — fixed by freeing WSL/Docker (~3.2 GB, Docker Desktop was idle) + `-j 1` (never two big crates at once) + `CMAKE_BUILD_PARALLEL_LEVEL=2` (CMake spawns internal jobs that ignore cargo `-j`).
> - **VERIFIED end-to-end on the installed build (2026-07-23):** MSI admin-extract + registry read (env var + PATH written, all files real/non-stub); installed app loads the bundled bge model from `C:\Program Files\Memory Vault\` (integrity OK); and a full fresh-first-run — moved dev vault + WebView state aside → empty vault + onboarding → reranker absent degrades cleanly (ADR-089) → first-run download of the 1.19 GB reranker from HF + SHA-256 verified → recall engine ready + warm with no relaunch (ADR-089/090).
> - **Open (non-blocking):** the File `@Source` is an absolute local path (portable/CI bundle must templatize); code signing deferred (SmartScreen warning expected for testers); blocker (f) release-hashing timing now measurable from a warm installed launch. The founder's dev vault is safe aside as `%APPDATA%/%LOCALAPPDATA%\com.shahbaz242630.memory-vault.dev-backup` (restore or delete at will — throwaway dogfood data).
>
> **📐 ADR-092 (session 26) — a dedicated cross-platform `vault-scheduler` crate: one `Scheduler` trait, three per-OS backends, per-user (no elevation). Implements the founder's 2026-07-23 pre-beta directive + BRD §2 / §5.6-scheduling.**
> - **Context:** the founder asked for a user-configurable automatic-maintenance schedule (its own tab + an onboarding page, Windows/macOS/Linux). Linux has no single scheduler; Windows has no cron. Modelled as a leaf capability per BRD §2 (traits, no cycles).
> - **Decision:** new leaf crate `vault-scheduler` depending only on `vault-core`. A **synchronous** `Scheduler` trait (`register`/`unregister`/`status`) with a compile-time-selected backend per target: **Windows** Task Scheduler via a **task-XML** definition through `schtasks /Create /XML` (NOT a `/TR` string — separates program from args, and lets us set `RunLevel=LeastPrivilege`+`InteractiveToken` = per-user no-elevation, and `StartWhenAvailable=true` = OS-level missed-run catch-up); **macOS** launchd (`~/Library/LaunchAgents` plist + `launchctl bootstrap gui/<uid>`; `StartCalendarInterval` wake-catch-up; native `EnvironmentVariables`); **Linux** systemd `--user` timer+service (`OnCalendar` + `Persistent=true` catch-up) **with a cron fallback** (single-quoted, no catch-up) when no systemd user session is reachable.
> - **Why synchronous + `spawn_blocking`:** registering a task is a short bounded subprocess call, not long I/O; the trait stays sync (no tokio dep in the leaf crate) and the async Tauri layer calls it via `spawn_blocking` — matches BRD §2's "blocking work is sync, dispatched via spawn_blocking".
> - **Cross-platform test coverage without dead code:** each backend module is `#[cfg(any(<os>, test))]` — a real build compiles only the native backend (no dead code, no `allow` hacks), but `cargo test` on ANY platform compiles + exercises all three PURE builders (the plist/XML/unit-file/crontab construction — where correctness + injection-safety live). Only the shell-out `imp` structs are gated to their exact OS.
> - **Windows LIVE-PROVEN (2026-07-24, founder's box):** an ignored round-trip integration test registered a real per-user task; Task Scheduler's own `/Query` reported it (`Next Run Time 3:00 AM`, `Logon Mode: Interactive only`, `Run As User: shahb`, program+args clean, 2-hour `ExecutionTimeLimit`); unregister removed it. mac/Linux `imp`s compile clean but their live `launchctl`/`systemctl`/`crontab` behaviour is CI/beta-pending — **NOT claimed proven**.
> - **New `VaultError::Scheduler(String)`** category in vault-core (scheduling is a first-class subsystem, not a Config/Io sub-case). Additive; the two DELIBERATELY-exhaustive matches in `vault-mcp` (`audit.rs::from_vault_error`, `server.rs::vault_error_to_mcp`) got a Scheduler arm (→ generic internal, since scheduling never reaches MCP dispatch). Spec-validation failures map to `InvalidInput`, not `Scheduler` — caught before any OS call. (Lesson banked: my first cascade check missed these two — they were in my own grep output; open the files, don't just list them.)
>
> **📐 ADR-093 (session 26) — the desktop app never loads Phi-4; both scheduled and manual consolidation spawn the bundled `vault-cli consolidate run` as a subprocess. Phi-4 downloads for everyone during onboarding, non-gating.**
> - **Context:** consolidation runs on Phi-4 (2.49 GB), and the desktop `AppConfig` had `phi4_model_path: None` (consolidator unwired). Wiring Phi-4 in-process would eagerly load 2.49 GB at `Application::new` on every launch — the exact startup cost ADR-090 fought.
> - **Decision:** the desktop app does NOT host Phi-4. Both the scheduled 3 AM run and the "Run now" button invoke the **bundled `vault-cli consolidate run`** (ADR-091 ships it) in its own short-lived process, pointed at the app's exact vault paths (`--vault-db <data>/vault.db --vector-dir <data>/lance --graph-db <data>/graph.duckdb consolidate --bge-model … --phi4-model … run` — **flags not env**, because Windows Task Scheduler cannot set per-task env). One consolidation code path for scheduled + manual; the app process stays light. This IS the documented V0.2 deployment.
> - **Single-writer safety (free):** `vault-cli consolidate` takes the vault-owner lock (ADR-SEC-002). If an MCP agent is connected (holds the lock), the run fails fast → surfaced as `maintenance_vault_busy` → UI says "will run at the next opportunity", never corrupts. The desktop UI itself is not a vault-owner, so "Run now" while the app is open is safe.
> - **Phi-4 acquisition (founder 2026-07-24 — "always during onboarding"):** `model_fetch::ensure_phi4[_with_progress]` mirrors the reranker downloader, referencing `vault_llm::Phi4MiniConfig`'s pinned constants (no restated hash). It downloads for EVERY user during onboarding but is **NON-gating** — the recall engine (reranker) still gates `finishOnboarding` because recall needs it; Phi-4 streams in the background because only maintenance needs it. `MaintenanceEngineFetch` (a second `OnceCell`) dedupes it; `maintenance-engine://progress` reports it.
> - **Trade-off / tech-debt:** every install downloads ~2.49 GB it may not use if the user never enables maintenance. Accepted per founder ("give every beta tester the real product ready") — background + non-gating. It currently fires on every launch (short-circuits if present), so a returning user who lacks it gets a background fetch; revisit if beta finds it heavy.
>
> **📐 ADR-SEC-005 (session 26) — scheduler injection-safety gate, no secrets on the command line, per-run audit, uninstall removal. BRD §11 re-read for the new subprocess + OS-registration surface.**
> - **The surface:** we register an OS task whose command line carries file paths, and (cron) execute through `/bin/sh`. The threat is a path/arg breaking out of its field to inject a directive or a second command.
> - **Injection-safety gate (tested FIRST, before any backend):** `TaskId::new` restricts the task id to `A-Za-z0-9._-` (excludes every separator / metacharacter / quote / whitespace); `ScheduleSpec::validate` rejects any CONTROL character in the label / args / env-values (newline = the crontab/plist/unit line-break injection vector) and constrains env-var NAMES to `A-Za-z0-9_`. Spaces + ordinary punctuation in PATHS are allowed and handled by per-backend escaping. Adversarial tests per backend: schtasks-XML entity-escape + Windows `CommandLineToArgvW` arg-quoting, launchd XML-escape, systemd `%`/`$` doubling + quote, POSIX single-quote (a `$(rm -rf ~)` arg survives only inside single quotes).
> - **No secrets on the command line:** the scheduled `vault-cli` reads the master key from the OS keychain at run time (verified in `build_application`); the spec carries only model paths + `LANCE_MEM_POOL_SIZE`. No key/passphrase is ever passed as an arg or env by us.
> - **Audit (§11.9.1):** `set_maintenance_schedule` (settings change) and `run_maintenance_now` (consolidation run) each write a `TauriCommandInvoke` audit row via the adapter (`ActorKind::User`). `get_maintenance_schedule` / `ensure_maintenance_engine` write none (read-only / no vault state — same posture as the engine commands).
> - **White-label (ADR-086):** every user-visible string, error code (`maintenance_*`), and event (`maintenance-engine://progress`) is stack-name-free; a `frontend_contract` test pins the event both ways.
> - **No security property weakened:** scheduling reads no memory and enforces no boundary itself; the consolidation it triggers runs the existing, already-audited consolidator. Verify-before-use unchanged. Matches ADR-088/089's standard.
> - **Uninstall (~~OPEN~~ → ✅ CLOSED session 30 by ADR-SEC-006, below):** the registered task is now removed on uninstall by a WiX custom action in the ADR-091 fragment. Code, guards and MSI-table verification are done; the live install/uninstall run is the session-31 opener.
>
> **📐 ADR-SEC-008 (session 31) — uninstall KEEPS your memories, the app says so plainly, and "Delete everything" cryptographically erases them. Same arc as ADR-SEC-007; the two ship together.**
> - **The question that started it (founder, 2026-08-14):** *"when the vault is uninstalled by the user — does data not get removed? or does it also get deleted?"* Answer, measured rather than assumed: **the installer never touches vault data.** `installer.wxs` has no `RemoveFile`/`RemoveFolder` for the data directory, and the folder read 3525.2 MB both before and after the ADR-SEC-006 uninstall — byte-identical. The Credential Manager entry survives too, so a reinstall restores a fully working vault (which is exactly what happened).
> - **INDUSTRY EVIDENCE (researched, not assumed).** Windows Installer's own rule is that an uninstall must *"leave no useless parts of itself behind"* — and it **explicitly carves out user data**, which persists unless the author writes a custom action to delete it. MSIX deletes everything; MSI does not. The cautionary tale is **Signal**, whose Windows uninstaller DID delete user data: a user lost their entire message history and argued *"user data should NEVER be deleted without the user's explicit permission"*; the issue was closed **not planned**. The closest analogue to us is **1Password** (encrypted local vault, key in Credential Manager) — it leaves vault caches behind and tells users to clear the Credential Manager entry by hand. **Obsidian** leaves data but offers an in-app "discard local database". Conclusion: **keeping data on uninstall is correct and near-universal; keeping it SILENTLY is the part we were getting wrong.**
> - **Decision (three parts):** (a) uninstall continues to leave data — deleting a year of memories as a side effect of a routine uninstall is unrecoverable loss, and people uninstall to troubleshoot; (b) the Settings tab now **states plainly** that uninstalling removes the app and not the memories, and shows the exact folder so the claim is checkable; (c) a deliberate **"Delete everything"** action performs cryptographic erasure. **Explicitly REJECTED: a "delete my data?" checkbox in the Windows uninstaller** — uninstall flows get click-through'd and the mistake is unrecoverable.
> - **The mechanism was already specified — BRD §11.5.4:** *"Account deletion: master key is destroyed, making all encrypted data permanently unrecoverable."* Since every at-rest key (`at_rest_key`, `sqlcipher_passphrase`) is a BLAKE3 subkey of the single 32-byte master_key in Credential Manager, destroying that key kills SQLCipher, the Lance store, the sealed graph AND the newly-sealed REPORTs at once. NIST SP 800-88 classes **cryptographic erase as Purge-level**, and unlike file deletion it is not defeated by journaling, SSD wear-levelling, block remapping, or a backup of the data folder taken last week.
> - **⚠️ THE ORDERING IS THE WHOLE DESIGN: destroy the KEY first, the FILES second.** The two orders fail completely differently under interruption, so this is a contract in `erasure.rs` rather than an implementation detail:
>
>   | order | interrupted halfway | result |
>   |---|---|---|
>   | key → files | files remain | data is cryptographically dead ✅ |
>   | files → key | key remains | any surviving copy still decrypts ❌ |
>
>   If the key step fails, **no files are touched at all** and the command returns an error: erasure either succeeds at the step that matters or does nothing.
> - **Erasure never opens the vault it is destroying.** `erase_vault` takes plain paths + identifiers, never an `Application` or a live `StorageBackend` — a wipe that requires a healthy vault is useless exactly when it is needed (corrupt database, half-migrated state, key that no longer matches the data). Founder-adjacent call taken inline and stated.
> - **`VAULT_ENTRIES` is an explicit allow-list, not a recursive delete of the vault directory.** The directory is caller-supplied, and a bug in that path must never become an unbounded `remove_dir_all` of, say, `%APPDATA%`. A test pins every entry to a single relative path component with no `..`. **`models/` is deliberately NOT erased** — ~3.5 GB of ML model files containing no user data; erasing memories must not force a multi-GB re-download.
> - **The delete step is VERIFIED, not assumed.** `destroy_master_key` re-probes the credential after deleting and returns an error if the secret is still readable. A `delete_credential` that silently did nothing would otherwise have us telling the user their vault was erased while every byte remained decryptable.
> - **NO audit row, and that is deliberate.** BRD §11.9.1 requires state-changing operations to be audited, but this operation destroys the database the audit log lives in — writing a row and then shredding its table records nothing. It is logged via `tracing` to the application log, which survives. Noted rather than silently skipped.
> - **UI: type-to-confirm, not a yes/no dialog.** The user types `DELETE`; the button is disabled until it matches exactly. A yes/no box is too easy to click through for an action that cannot be undone even from a backup. `frontend_contract.rs` pins the gate — and pins that the phrase check appears **before** the `invoke`, since a guard that runs after the call is decorative. (Session 21's lesson still applies underneath: this webview renders native `confirm` as OK-only, so native dialogs cannot gate anything.)
> - **The failure message tells the truth.** If erasure fails the UI says the memories were **NOT** deleted and are **still readable** — softer wording would be a lie about the single property the user was trying to obtain. Undeletable leftover files are reported as a *disk-space* fact and explicitly do **not** downgrade the confidentiality result, because once the key is gone those bytes are unreadable ciphertext.
> - **ADR-086 carve-out applies here.** Naming AES-256 and the OS credential store is permitted for security specifics, and on this surface it is *owed*: a user destroying their data is entitled to know what "deleted" actually means.
>
> **📐 ADR-SEC-007 (session 31) — the consolidator REPORT is SEALED at rest. It was writing verbatim memory text to disk in the clear, in violation of BRD §11.5.1's "no exceptions".**
> - **How it was found — it was NOT a code review.** The founder asked a product question: *"when the vault is uninstalled, does the data get removed?"* Answering it honestly meant checking what is actually on disk after an uninstall, and the sweep of `%APPDATA%\com.shahbaz242630.memory-vault` returned: `vault.db` ✅ encrypted (SQLCipher — header is random bytes, not `SQLite format 3`), `graph.sealed` ✅ encrypted, the whole `lance/` vector store ✅ encrypted, `.vault.lock` + `maintenance.json` plaintext but content-free — and **`reports/personal.report.json` PLAINTEXT, containing verbatim fact text, memory ids, `as_of` timestamps and confidences.** Read with a plain `Get-Content`, no key involved. **Generalisable: a question about product behaviour walked straight onto a security bug that no amount of reading the security section would have found, because §11 says what MUST be true and the disk says what IS true.**
> - **The violated rule is unambiguous.** BRD §11.5.1: *"All data on disk is encrypted. No exceptions."* Not a judgement call, not a trade-off — a spec violation. The threat model covers it explicitly (§11.1, "Compromised endpoints — Access our app's data files on disk").
> - **ROOT CAUSE — a checklist-coverage gap, not carelessness.** BRD §11.12's *"No plaintext data on disk, ever"* is listed under **vault-storage**. The REPORT is written by **vault-consolidator** and read by **vault-retrieval**, neither of which carries that line. The artifact was born in crates whose security checklist never mentioned at-rest encryption, so every individual crate was compliant with its own checklist while the vault as a whole was not. **Per-crate checklists cannot catch an artifact that crosses crates.** (Tech-debt (f) below proposes the workspace-wide guard.)
> - **Decision: reuse the EXISTING envelope, add no crypto (SP-5).** `vault_storage::{seal_vault_blob, unseal_vault_blob}` are thin public wrappers over the already-shipped `seal_file_bytes`/`unseal_file_bytes` — the same XChaCha20-Poly1305 dryoc envelope, the same `version || granularity || header || ciphertext` framing, the same `"vault-at-rest-v1"` domain separator that seals the vector store and the graph. There is no second crypto path to review.
> - **The boundary binding came free, which is the neat part.** AAD is already `BLAKE3("vault-at-rest-v1" || relative_path)`, and the REPORT's relative path is `reports/<boundary>.report.sealed` — so **the boundary name is inside the AAD by construction**. That satisfies BRD §11.3.2 ("AAD includes … boundary … prevents swap attacks") with no new mechanism: copying `work.report.sealed` over `personal.report.sealed` changes the AAD, AEAD authentication fails, and the read fails closed instead of serving one boundary's facts under another's name — which would have defeated the mandatory access control in §11.4.3.
> - **Layout moved DOWN to `vault_storage::report_layout`, and that is a correctness decision rather than tidiness.** The filename and the AAD string are needed by the writer (`vault-consolidator`) and the reader (`vault-retrieval`), which do not depend on each other; `report_io.rs` had therefore hand-duplicated `REPORTS_DIRNAME` with a comment explaining why. That was fine while the filename was the only shared fact. **Once the path became AAD input, a one-character divergence between the two copies would seal fine, write fine, and then fail EVERY unseal — degrading silently to `REPORT_MISSING` on every read, with no error at the point of the mistake and a green build.** One definition, in the crate both sides already depend on, removes the failure mode by construction. The original "no sibling dependency" rationale is preserved: `vault-storage` is a shared lower layer, not a sibling.
> - **The extension changed `.report.json` → `.report.sealed`, deliberately.** A `.json` file that is really an encrypted envelope invites a future reader to `serde_json` it, fail, and "fix" the failure with a plaintext fallback — reintroducing this exact bug. Distinct extensions make the two formats impossible to confuse, and a test asserts the path does not end in `.json`.
> - **NO plaintext fallback on the read path, ever.** Unseal failure (wrong key / tampering / wrong boundary) logs at ERROR and returns `Ok(None)`, degrading through the existing `REPORT_MISSING` path — because an unauthenticatable REPORT is exactly as trustworthy as no REPORT (SP-4). A legacy `.report.json` left on disk is **not** read as a fallback, pinned by `legacy_plaintext_report_is_never_read`.
> - **MIGRATION IS THE HALF THAT ACTUALLY CLOSES THE HOLE.** Shipping the sealed writer alone fixes only future runs: the new writer uses a different filename, so every existing `reports/<boundary>.report.json` would sit there in the clear forever. `migrate_plaintext_reports` runs on **every** startup (idempotent; no version marker, because a marker that got out of step would silently skip the sweep and the sweep is cheap), re-seals the content where it can, and **deletes the plaintext unconditionally — including on the failure path**. An unreadable REPORT is worthless, and preserving a worthless file in the clear is strictly worse than losing it; the next consolidation regenerates it. It also destroys leftover `.report.json.tmp` files, which carry the same fact text and which a crash-mid-write leaves behind.
> - **Founder decisions taken inline (both were mine to call, stated not blocked on):** (1) migrate by SEALING rather than plain deletion, so recall quality is not degraded until the next consolidation; (2) the forthcoming delete-everything action must work even when the vault cannot be opened — a wipe that needs a healthy vault is useless exactly when it is needed.
> - **Tests are security-first per BRD §11.13** — adversarial before functional: wrong key, tampered ciphertext, truncated envelope, cross-boundary swap, legacy-plaintext-never-read, migration-destroys-plaintext-even-when-unresealable. **The load-bearing one is `sealed_bytes_do_not_contain_the_plaintext` / `written_report_is_not_readable_on_disk`:** a sealing wrapper that silently did nothing would pass every round-trip test ever written, so the suite asserts the property the feature exists for — the secret is not in the bytes — and each such test carries a control proving it is testing encryption rather than an empty file. Same discipline as ADR-SEC-006's comment-stripping guard, which passed on prose until it was made to run against real markup.
> - **Scope — stated, not silently skipped:** this ADR covers the REPORT only. `maintenance.json` (schedule config) and `.vault.lock` remain plaintext and are content-free by inspection; they are noted, not fixed. Part B (crypto-shred delete-everything + honest uninstall messaging) is the same arc and lands with this work.
>
> **📐 ADR-SEC-006 (session 30) — uninstall removes the registered maintenance task via a WiX custom action driving `schtasks`, not our own binary. Closes ADR-SEC-005's last open item.**
> - **The gap was live, not theoretical.** On the founder's machine `\com.memoryvault.maintenance` was **still registered** from a session-27 test: `Status: Ready` · `Next Run Time 29/07/2026 5:25 AM` · `Last Run 28/07/2026 10:05:51` · **`Last Result: 1`** · `Task To Run: C:\Projects\GitHub\Memory Vault\target\debug\vault-cli.exe …`. A task that outlived whatever created it by weeks, firing daily into a repo path we have deleted and rebuilt several times, failing every morning, and nobody knew. A beta tester who installs, tries the feature and uninstalls gets exactly this. For a product whose promise is "you own your data and nothing is left behind", leaving an OS-level job behind is squarely the wrong behaviour.
> - **Decision:** a `<CustomAction>` in `windows/fragments/installer.wxs` runs `"[SystemFolder]schtasks.exe" /Delete /TN "com.memoryvault.maintenance" /F` during uninstall.
> - **WHY `schtasks` AND NOT our own `vault-cli`** (the tempting choice, since `vault_scheduler::Scheduler::unregister` is already written and tested): **during an uninstall our binary is the thing being deleted.** Driving removal through it means sequencing before `RemoveFiles`, and it could be file-locked, already gone, or crash — and a crash there fails the USER'S UNINSTALL. `schtasks.exe` ships with Windows, is always present, and is unaffected by our own removal. vault-scheduler still owns removal at RUN time; this is purely the teardown path, where that crate is unavailable by construction.
> - **WHY `NOT UPGRADINGPRODUCTCODE` — the non-obvious one, and the reason this needed thought rather than a one-liner.** `REMOVE="ALL"` is **also true while a major upgrade removes the OLD product**. Conditioned on `REMOVE` alone, **every app update would silently switch the user's automatic maintenance off and never say so** — a data-adjacent behaviour change with no notification. `UPGRADINGPRODUCTCODE` is set only during that removal-during-upgrade pass, so excluding it preserves the schedule across upgrades and clears it only on a real uninstall.
> - **`Return="ignore"`:** `schtasks /Delete` exits non-zero when the task is absent (never scheduled, or already removed). That must never fail an uninstall — matching the idempotent posture `unregister` already has in Rust.
> - **`Execute="immediate"`, not deferred:** the task is per-user, created by the interactive user with `LogonType=InteractiveToken` (verified session 27), so removal belongs in that user's context; immediate execution also resolves `[SystemFolder]` normally, which a deferred action cannot. **Accepted limit:** if user A installs and user B uninstalls, A's task survives. Deferred-as-SYSTEM would cover it but cannot resolve properties. Single-owner desktop vault — not worth the complexity today, logged as tech debt (b4).
> - **✅ VERIFIED IN THE BUILT MSI, not merely in source** — the fragment could have compiled perfectly and still been dropped (see the next bullet). Read back out of the shipped `.msi` via the `WindowsInstaller.Installer` COM API:
>
>   | table | row |
>   |---|---|
>   | `CustomAction` | `RemoveMaintenanceTask \| 98 \| INSTALLDIR \| "[SystemFolder]schtasks.exe" /Delete /TN "com.memoryvault.maintenance" /F` |
>   | `InstallExecuteSequence` | `RemoveMaintenanceTask \| REMOVE="ALL" AND NOT UPGRADINGPRODUCTCODE \| 3499` |
>   | `InstallExecuteSequence` | `RemoveFiles \| 3500` |
>
>   Type **98** = exe(2) + directory(32) + continue-on-error(64). Runs at 3499, one step before file removal at 3500, exactly as designed.
> - **⚠️ A TRAPDOOR FOUND UNDER THE PACKAGING WE ALREADY SHIPPED:** a WiX `<Fragment>` is compiled in **only because something references it** — here `tauri.conf.json`'s `bundle.windows.wix.componentRefs`. Rename a component on either side and the **entire** fragment (the `LANCE_MEM_POOL_SIZE` env var, the PATH entry, `vault-cli.exe` itself, and now the uninstall action) vanishes from the MSI **with no error anywhere**. That was already true before this change and nothing guarded it. `installer_contract.rs` now pins every `componentRef` to a real `Component Id`, and pins the removal action into the same single fragment.
> - **🔬 THE FINDING WORTH KEEPING — my own guard failed on my own work, and it was right to.** First run: *"installer.wxs now has 2 `<Fragment>` elements"*. Cause: **the explanatory comment I had just written contains the literal `<Fragment>`**. Pulling the thread showed something worse — `installer.wxs` documents itself heavily, so its comments quote the very strings the guards search for (the task id, `Return="ignore"`). Scanning the raw file, **three of the four guards would have passed on prose alone: the uninstall command could have been deleted outright and the suite would still have gone green, because the paragraph explaining it survived.** Fixed at the mechanism rather than by rewording: every guard runs against comment-stripped markup, the stripper has its own non-vacuity test, and the fix was **negative-verified by sabotage** — breaking the real `ExeCommand` to `com.memoryvault.WRONG` while leaving the comment intact makes guard 1 FAIL (evidence: `C:\Projects\MemoryVault-artifacts\adr-sec-006-guard-negative-verification.log`). **Generalisable: a guard that scans a self-documenting file must strip the documentation first, or the documentation satisfies the guard.**
> - **Scope — stated, not silently skipped:** Windows only. macOS and Linux ship no installer yet, so there is no uninstall hook to attach to; their teardown lands with their packaging.
> - **✅ LIVE-VERIFIED 2026-08-14 (session 31) — PASS. This closes ADR-SEC-006 and, with it, ADR-SEC-005's last open item.** Run elevated on the founder's machine, 14:50:07–14:50:31 (24s, not the forecast 5–10 min). Evidence: `C:\Projects\MemoryVault-artifacts\uninstall-verify-logs\` (`uninstall-verify.txt` + three full `msiexec` verbose logs).
>
>   | step | expected | observed |
>   |---|---|---|
>   | 0 · baseline | task PRESENT | **PRESENT** |
>   | 1 · uninstall OLD build `{7BDA7062…}` (no removal action) — **NEGATIVE CONTROL** | task SURVIVES | **PRESENT** — the gap was real |
>   | 2 · install NEW build `{1E26A76E…}` | task untouched | **PRESENT** |
>   | 3 · uninstall NEW build — **THE TEST** | task ABSENT | **ABSENT** |
>
> - **Verified at MECHANISM level, not just outcome** — an absent task at the end is also what "the task was never there" looks like, so the `msiexec` logs were read directly: `msi-uninstall-new.log` contains `Doing action: RemoveMaintenanceTask` → `Action ended … Return value 1`; `msi-uninstall-old.log` contains **zero** occurrences (negative control fired nothing); `msi-install-new.log` has **zero** `Doing action` hits, confirming the `REMOVE="ALL"` condition kept it off the install path. End state re-checked independently of the script's own reporting: task absent, install dir gone, **0** registered products, and **vault data intact at 3.5 GB** in `%APPDATA%\com.shahbaz242630.memory-vault`.
> - **🔬 THE SESSION-30 DIAGNOSIS OF WHY THIS DIDN'T RUN WAS WRONG, AND THE REAL CAUSE IS A TRAP THAT WILL RECUR.** Session 30 recorded *"the UAC prompt was never accepted, so nothing ran at all"* and warned future sessions off `Start-Process -Verb RunAs`. **Elevation was never the problem.** `uninstall-verify.ps1` was **UTF-8 with no BOM and contained em-dashes** (`—` = `E2 80 94`). PowerShell 5.1 loads BOM-less files as **CP1252**, in which byte `0x94` is a **right curly quote `”`** — and the PowerShell tokenizer **accepts curly quotes as string delimiters**. Every em-dash therefore closed its string mid-line: `powershell.exe -File` failed to parse and exited before executing a single statement. Reproduced this session — `PSParser::Tokenize` reported 5 syntax errors (lines 38, 46, 57, 58, 59), all on lines pairing an em-dash with a `$(…)` subexpression. The launch had always been fine; the script was unparseable. **The `-Verb RunAs` warning in the session-30 opener is retracted: it worked first try here, with a UAC prompt the founder accepted.** (The em-dashes are provably pre-existing — they are visible in this session's read of the file *before* any edit. Whether a BOM was also lost is unproven, but the file was authored by tooling that writes BOM-less UTF-8, so it was almost certainly never parseable.)
> - **Generalisable, and it cost two sessions:** **any PowerShell script preserved for a later session must be pure ASCII** (or written with a BOM). Our prose style — em-dashes everywhere — is actively hostile to PowerShell 5.1 script files. `uninstall-verify.ps1` is now pure ASCII (0 non-ASCII bytes, verified) and carries a header saying why. **And the deeper lesson, which is the ADR-098 lesson again in a new costume: "it launched" is not "it ran."** Session 30 checked for side-effects and correctly concluded nothing had happened, but then attributed it to the most plausible-sounding cause instead of instrumenting. A marker file written on the first line of an elevated wrapper distinguishes *never started* from *started and died* in one run — that wrapper is preserved at `C:\Projects\MemoryVault-artifacts\run-elevated-wrapper.ps1`.
> - **Hardened while fixing it, because a half-run was a live risk:** step 1 uninstalls the OLD build *before* step 2 installs the new one, so a missing or wrong MSI would have left the founder with **no app installed and a void test**. The script now runs a pre-flight that aborts before touching anything unless it is elevated, finds an MSI (build tree, falling back to the preserved copy so a wiped `target/` costs no 71-minute respin), **and confirms via the `WindowsInstaller` COM API that the MSI actually contains `RemoveMaintenanceTask`** — otherwise it is the old build and step 3 would fail for the wrong reason. That check earns its keep: both MSIs are named `Memory Vault_0.2.0_x64_en-US.msi` and are distinguishable only by ProductCode and 86 KB of size.
> - ⚠️ **The app is left UNINSTALLED by this run** (by design — step 3 is an uninstall). Vault data is untouched. Reinstall with: `msiexec /i "C:\Projects\MemoryVault-artifacts\Memory Vault_0.2.0_ADR-SEC-006_uninstall-removes-task.msi"` — needed before the onboarding-step-4 replay.
>
> **📐 ADR-094 (session 27) — the Maintenance tab becomes "Consolidation": three explained cards, user-facing rename only. Amends ADR-086 (UI content policy).**
> - **Context:** live-verifying the session-26 UI (2026-07-25) surfaced a usability failure no test could. The founder set a time, pressed **Save schedule**, and nothing was scheduled — because the opt-in checkbox was never ticked. The backend was correct throughout (`enabled=false` → `unregister` → success → persist), and the *silence* in the log was the evidence that found it. A button reading "Save schedule" that means "turn it off" when a nearby checkbox is clear is a trap every beta user will hit the same way.
> - **Decision (founder, 2026-07-25):** (a) rename the user-facing surface **Maintenance → Consolidation**; (b) split the single panel into **three numbered cards** — ① consolidate automatically, ② when it runs, ③ run one now — each carrying a 2–3 line plain-English explainer so no control is a bare label the user has to guess at; (c) "Run one now" leaves card ② entirely and gets its own card **and its own status line** (`maint-run-note`), so a run's outcome can never be reported under "Save schedule".
> - **Why "Consolidation", not "Consolidator":** the tab names an activity, not a machine. It also kills a live inconsistency — the tab said "Maintenance" while the run output displayed directly beneath it said *"starting consolidation run…"*.
> - **The rename is USER-FACING ONLY — deliberate, do not "fix" it.** Tauri command names (`*_maintenance_*`), the progress event (`maintenance-engine://progress`), the error codes (`maintenance_*`), `<data>/maintenance.json`, the tab key and every element id stay `maintenance*`. Renaming them changes no behaviour and would churn the `frontend_contract` pins for nothing. **Accepted consequence: the code and the UI now use different words for the same thing.**
> - **Does NOT weaken ADR-086:** "Consolidation" names no model, vendor, or runtime — the white-label rule targets stack exposure, and this is our own domain vocabulary. The explainers now carry the comprehension load that the shorter, vaguer "Maintenance" was silently failing to carry.
> - **Last-run line fixed:** it rendered `summary.split("\n")[0]` — the first line of `vault-cli`'s stdout, which is literally *"starting consolidation run..."*, the least informative line in the report. `summariseRun()` now parses the report's counts → *"nothing needed tidying"* or *"merged 3 duplicates, flagged 2 contradictions, archived 1 old memory"*, and returns empty (rendering nothing) on an unrecognised format rather than guessing. Verified against the exact stdout this machine produced at 10:45, plus singular/plural and unparseable cases.
> - **Rode along — the onboarding step count was wrong.** Session 26 added a 4th onboarding step but left the earlier kickers reading **"STEP 2 OF 3"** and **"STEP 3 OF 3"**, so a user would reach "step 3 of 3" and then be shown a fourth screen. Both corrected to `OF 4`. Found by reading the file, not by clicking.
>
> **📐 ADR-095 (session 27) — the retry worker drains its queue on shutdown instead of stopping mid-queue; `Application::start` is renamed `spawn_retry_worker`. Found by live-testing ADR-092/093.**
> - **Context — how it surfaced:** proving the consolidation path needed real duplicate data, so a harness wrote 7 facts over MCP and disconnected. The consolidation then found **cluster_count=0** and the worker's own `NN search returned zero rows` warning fired 7 times. `vault-cli divergence-check` explained it: **sqlite 7 / vector 0**. Not a consolidator bug at all — the vectors were never written.
> - **Root cause:** `StorageBackend::write_memory` writes SQLite + `retry_queue` synchronously and returns; the VECTOR write is performed later by `RetryWorker` off that queue (~1.8s per entry, measured). `RetryWorker::run` checked `cancel` at the top of its loop and `break`'d immediately, so everything still queued at shutdown was left for whenever a worker next ran. Live: 24 memories written, disconnect after ~10s → **13 of 24 had no vector**; hold the session open the full **43s** → **24/24 landed**.
> - **NOT data loss — corrected mid-session.** `retry_queue` is a durable SQLite table, so the work survives process exit and resumes on the next start. Proof: the dev vault read **7 sqlite / 0 vector** at 14:45 and **7 / 1** at 16:36 — one entry completed unattended during a later session. The defect is therefore **unbounded completion time**, not loss. My first two characterisations of this to the founder said "silently lost … permanently"; that was wrong and is retracted here.
> - **Why it still matters:** until the vector lands, the memory is invisible to semantic recall AND to the consolidator's clustering (it cannot seed or be an edge), while keyword search keeps returning it — so the vault looks healthy. That masking is the dangerous part, not the delay.
> - **Decision:** on cancel, `RetryWorker::run` hands to `drain(budget)` — work the queue until empty or `DEFAULT_DRAIN_TIMEOUT` (60s). **Polls on queue LENGTH, not on the first `StepResult::Idle`:** `enqueue` sets `next_attempt_at` up to ~1.25s ahead, so at the instant of shutdown a just-written cascade is present-but-not-due and an `Idle`-terminated drain would exit instantly, deferring exactly the entries it exists to flush. Budget chosen from the measurement (24 → 43s). Exhausting it logs **WARN with the remaining count** — the condition becomes visible rather than silent. `ApplicationHandle::shutdown` already awaited the worker and already called it a "graceful drain"; the comment was aspirational, the code now matches it.
> - **Rename `start()` → `spawn_retry_worker()` (16 call sites).** It was documented as a **"test-focused entry point"** while being the ONLY lifecycle a GUI can use (`start_with_mcp` blocks on an MCP handshake that never arrives in a Tauri app — ADR-034), so both `vault-tauri` and `vault-cli daemon` have always run production through it. ADR-090 catalogued three defects traced to that divergence; this drain gap is the fourth, and the mislabel is why "does the desktop drain on close?" was awkward to even ask. The honest split is by COMPOSITION (worker-alone vs worker+MCP+signals), not test-vs-production.
> - **Desktop app deliberately NOT changed.** `spawn_retry_worker` drops the worker's `JoinHandle`, so a Tauri caller can signal but cannot await a drain; fixing it needs an API change plus an exit hook that would block window-close on disk I/O. The desktop already self-heals (the worker spawns every launch and users launch often), so the trade is bad: a visibly frozen close for a condition that resolves itself. Logged as tech debt, not done.
>
> **📐 ADR-096 (session 28) — deterministic dedup gains a third, ORDER-SENSITIVE axis; the documented "contradictions never reach dedup" premise is FALSIFIED. Amends ADR-063.**
> - **Context:** chasing session 27's open finding required measuring where each semantic class actually sits. `crates/vault-embedding/examples/paraphrase_cluster_gate.rs` (new, rides with this commit) reproduces Phase-1 clustering exactly — same top-5 NN rule, same union-find, same threshold arithmetic — and **self-validates before any conclusion is drawn from it**: it predicts all 12 outcomes of the live 2026-07-25 battery run and refuses to be trusted if even one disagrees.
> - **The falsified premise, quoted verbatim from the code it corrects:** *"Contradictory pairs can share high containment but score < 0.92 cosine, so they never cluster at the 0.92 gate AND are caught separately by the topic-level A5 pass — the cosine axis keeps them out of dedup."* **Measured across 8 contradictory pairs: 0.7199 – 0.9979**, four above the documented 0.883 ceiling (laptop MacBook/ThinkPad 0.8820 · deploy AWS/Azure 0.8842 · standup 9am/10:30am 0.9042 · Berlin twice/four-times 0.9042). The 2026-05-31 calibration sampled only *different-value* contradictions (Vega/Atlas, measured 0.7199 here); *reversed-argument* contradictions were never in its sample.
> - **The defect that premise hid.** `"prefers tea over coffee"` / `"prefers coffee over tea"` → cosine **0.9979** (above every true paraphrase) and containment **1.00** (identical word set). Both dedup axes are order-blind *by construction*, so the pair cleared both and was collapsed by plain code — **no model call, no contradiction record**.
> - **Live evidence (2026-07-26), clean 4-memory vault, preserved `b223f34` binary:** 4 in / 3 out · `cluster_count=1` · `clusters deduped: 1` · `memories deduped: 1` · `contradictions queued: 0` · `contradictions_auto_resolved=0` · summary `## Merges` EMPTY, `**Auto-resolved (newer fact won):** 0`. Both unrelated controls untouched, so the effect was specific, not general breakage.
> - **Decision:** third axis `is_pure_reordering(a, b)` — same token MULTISET, different order → decline the deterministic collapse. Checked **last**, after both similarity gates pass, so its `info!` marks precisely a collapse that would otherwise have been silent. **Logs memory IDs only, never content** (vault text must not reach logs).
> - **Deliberately narrow:** fires only on a pure permutation. Battery pair 1 (`code editor` / `code editors`) is a different multiset, so genuine near-duplicates still collapse — pinned by `plan_dedup_still_collapses_a_genuine_near_duplicate`.
> - **False positives are cheap by design.** A same-words-different-order pair that IS a paraphrase (`"Mondays and Thursdays"` / `"Thursdays and Mondays"`) is not lost — it merely stops qualifying for the *deterministic* collapse and goes to Phase-2 judgement, which can still merge it. An unnecessary model call costs seconds; a blind-collapsed contradiction costs a fact.
> - **Re-verified live on the fixed build**, same 4 facts, fresh vault: guard logged · `clusters deduped: 0` / `memories deduped: 0` · `contradictions_auto_resolved=1` · summary `**Auto-resolved (newer fact won):** 1` + per-boundary detail.
> - **⚠️ A FRAMING CORRECTION MADE MID-SESSION, before it could mislead.** My first write-up cited BRD §5.6 line 985 (*"For contradictions, write to `ConflictReview` queue, do not auto-resolve"*) as though auto-resolution were forbidden. Checking the archives falsified that: `Memory Vault Tests.md` K6 specifies *"clear-winner contradictions auto-`invalidate()`; ambiguous ones queue to `conflicts_for_user_review`, not silently picked"* — auto-resolving a clear winner is **intended**. **The defect was never that a fact was retired; it was that the pair was filed as a DUPLICATE, so contradiction detection never ran on it at all.** The module doc, the log string and the test comment were all corrected in the same commit. Lesson: verify what a locked artefact actually says before building a framing on it.
> - **Rode along — dedup collapses rendered NOWHERE in the run summary.** `write_merges_section` rendered only `applied_merges`, so a dedup-only run printed an empty `## Merges`. The counts already existed (`BoundarySummary::deduped_{clusters,memories}` → `ConsolidationReport`) and were printed by the CLI — purely a rendering gap in the artefact a user reads. Now emits `**Collapsed as duplicates:** N` + per-boundary detail, mirroring the Contradictions section's Finding-A line. Same principle: **a collapse you cannot see is one you cannot decide to roll back.**
> - **Does NOT change** the 0.92 clustering gate, the 0.93 / 0.80 dedup floors, or any merge outcome for non-permutation pairs.
>
> **🔎 RE-OPENED (session 28) — the 0.92 gate is TRACTABLE. My "threshold cannot fix it" conclusion is RETRACTED, and the replacement number is 0.89.**
> - **Measured class ranges** (same self-validating instrument): **paraphrase 0.8237 – 0.9936 · contradictory 0.7199 – 0.9979 · complementary 0.5418 – 0.7943 · cross-case 0.3737 – 0.9289.**
> - **Why the overlap is NOT fatal (the retraction).** I first read paraphrase/contradictory overlap as ruling out any threshold move. It does not, because **clustering is not where facts die — deterministic dedup is.** Blind collapse requires cosine ≥ `NEAR_IDENTICAL_COS` (0.93); anything scoring ≥0.93 **already clusters at 0.92**. So lowering the gate can only add pairs in `[new, 0.92)`, every one of which is *below* the dedup floor and therefore **must** go to judgement. Confirmed empirically, not just argued: `contraBLIND` is **0 at every threshold from 0.96 down to 0.80**, and `paraBLIND` is flat at 4.
> - **Sweep over the full 56-memory corpus** (`para · paraBLIND · contra · contraBLIND · comple · chained`):
>   **0.92 → 7 · 4 · 1 · 0 · 0 · 1** (shipped) · **0.90 → 8 · 4 · 3 · 0 · 0 · 1** · **0.89 → 9 · 4 · 3 · 0 · 0 · 1** · **0.84 → 10 · 4 · 6 · 0 · 0 · 1** · **0.82 → 10 · 4 · 5 · 0 · 0 · 2**.
> - **0.82 is the floor** — `chained` doubles there, the first sign of unrelated facts gluing together via transitive closure.
> - **DANGER SCAN CLEAN.** The shape that would sink this — a NON-permutation contradiction that still clears cosine 0.93 + containment 0.80, which no threshold and no order guard would catch — **does not exist in the 16 non-paraphrase pairs measured**. Evidence, not proof; the scan is built into the example and re-runs free.
> - **THE ONE THING THE SWEEP CANNOT MODEL, and the reason to prove 0.89 live before trusting it:** `would_blind_collapse` reproduces the *deterministic* path only. It proves nothing is collapsed **silently**. It does **not** prove the model declines to merge a contradiction once handed one. If Phi-4 merges `"standup is at 9am"` with `"standup is at 10:30am"`, a fact is lost — recorded, but lost. That is the acceptance criterion for the live run.
> - **Two pairs are out of reach at any safe threshold** and should be accepted as such: `"is a vegetarian"` / `"does not eat meat"` (0.8237) and `"prefers short, direct answers"` / `"likes concise, straightforward replies"` (0.8452).
>
> **📐 ADR-097 (session 29) — the shipped clustering gate is 0.84, diverging deliberately from BRD §5.6 line 904's 0.92. Resolves the RE-OPENED analysis above.**
> - ⚠️ **Heading corrected session 30:** this ADR was titled *"the shipped clustering gate is 0.89"* while its own body, the live-run table and `consolidator.rs:208` all say **0.84**. 0.89 was the value the plan intended to ship before the founder-directed live test retired the deferral (see the ⚖️ bullet below); the heading never caught up. **The shipped value is 0.84 — verified in code, not inferred.** Some bullets below still reason in 0.89 terms where they describe that intermediate step; that is history, not the current value. Corrected because a future session reading only the heading of a correctness-critical gate would draw the wrong conclusion.
> - **Decision:** `ConsolidatorConfig::default().merge_similarity_threshold` **0.92 → 0.84**. This is the first deliberate numeric divergence from a BRD §5.6 default, so it is pinned by an assertion with the reason in the message (`consolidator_config_default_matches_brd_spec`) rather than left to drift.
> - **The evidence the prior analysis was missing — the 100-memory ship-gate.** The re-opened sweep above reasoned over 28 hand-written pairs. The BRD §6.2 clustering ship-gate is graded on a different, larger artefact: the 100-entry / 20-topic labelled fixture behind `tests/acceptance.rs`, with ADR-045 §d floors precision ≥ 0.95 and recall ≥ 0.90. That is the test a threshold move is most likely to break, and finding out costs a ~35-minute crate run — so **Phase C was added to the diagnostic to sweep it in seconds first**:
>
>   | gate | precision | recall | verdict |
>   |---|---|---|---|
>   | 0.93 | 1.0000 | 0.8750 | **FAIL** (recall) |
>   | 0.92 | 1.0000 | 0.9300 | PASS ← was shipped |
>   | **0.89** | **1.0000** | **0.9650** | **PASS ← now shipped** |
>   | 0.84 | 1.0000 | 1.0000 | PASS |
>
>   **Precision is a perfect 1.0000 at every value from 0.96 down to 0.80** — on this fixture the move costs nothing and buys recall only. It also shows 0.92 was sitting closer to the recall floor (0.93 vs 0.90) than anyone had noticed, one step from failing its own ship-gate. **At the shipped 0.84, recall is perfect too (1.0000).**
> - **✅ THE MODEL WAS THEN CONFIRMED BY THE REAL TEST, EXACTLY.** `cargo test -p vault-consolidator --test acceptance -- --nocapture` at 0.89: `threshold=0.89` · **`precision=1.0`** (floor 0.95) · **`recall=0.965`** (floor 0.90) · Gate A `nn_shared_topic=100` (floor 90). The sweep predicted 1.0000 / 0.9650 and the storage-backed run returned 1.0 / 0.965 — the diagnostic reproduces `find_candidate_clusters` (ANN via LanceDB) to the printed digit, so its numbers can be trusted for the NEXT gate decision without paying the crate-test cost first.
> - **⚠️ 0.82 IS THE FLOOR, AND 0.84 SITS ON IT.** At 0.82 the `chained` count doubles — clusters begin spanning more than one case by transitive closure, i.e. unrelated facts gluing together. 0.84 is the last value before that starts. **Do not go lower without new evidence**; the next reduction would also push the below-gate NN test fixture (0.8196) above the gate and silently retire that coverage.
> - **⚖️ WHY 0.84 AND NOT 0.89 — the deferral was retired the same session, by testing it.** The plan shipped 0.89 and parked 0.84 on the grounds that it "pulls 6 contradictions into the merge path vs 3, and the unmeasured risk is model behaviour". **Founder's call was to test it rather than assume it** — the right call, and it changed the outcome. Once Phi-4's conduct was *observed* rather than projected (3/3 declined at 0.89), the argument for parking 0.84 evaporated: it was caution about an unknown that was no longer unknown. Re-tested at 0.84 with **six** contradictions and it declined all six. **0.84 dominates 0.89 on every measured axis** — recall 0.9650 → 1.0000, same perfect precision, same zero blind-collapses, one extra same-meaning pair rescued. **Lesson worth keeping: "we haven't tested it" is a reason to test, not a reason to ship the weaker value.**
> - **⚠️ THE FINDING THAT WAS NOT IN THE PLAN — a ship-gate test's PREMISE was silently invalidated.** `contradiction_resolution.rs` asserted `cos < 0.92` as a *premise*: Vega/Atlas must sit **below** the merge gate, which is the entire reason the A5 nearest-neighbour pass has to exist. **Measured, that pair is 0.9049** — so at 0.89 (and at 0.90) it clusters and the premise is FALSE. Caught by adding Phase D to the diagnostic, which measures the exact strings pinned in tests against candidate gates. Had it not been caught: the test fails on its premise line, and "fixing" the number would have made it pass **for the wrong reason** — its single-canned-response mock returns contradiction-judge JSON, which `decide_merge` cannot parse, so the cluster is logged-and-skipped and Phase 2b catches the pair anyway. Green, via a parse failure, proving nothing about the path it now takes.
> - **How it is handled** (all three consequences, not just the compile error):
>   1. **Premises are computed against the shipped gate, never hardcoded** — `shipped_gate()` reads `ConsolidatorConfig::default()`. A literal is what let this drift go unseen; the same mistake cannot silently recur.
>   2. **`PhaseAwareMock`** dispatches on the requested JSON schema (`"shared_attribute"` → Phase 2b judge, else → Phase 2 merge) so a pair that now passes through BOTH phases gets a valid answer from each. It deliberately answers `contradiction` with **no** `clear_winner` — the weaker branch, leaving both facts active — so the burden stays on Phase 2b and the tests still prove what their names claim.
>   3. **`below_gate_contradiction_is_caught_only_by_the_nearest_neighbour_pass`** (new) restores the coverage the old premise carried, on a fixture **measured** at 0.8196 — below the 0.89 gate so Phase 1 forms no edge, above the 0.70 `CONTRADICTION_NN_SIMILARITY_FLOOR` so the judge is still offered it. Both margins are ~100x the sub-1e-3 cross-platform embedding noise.
> - **Why admitting contradictions to the merge path is safe** (the question the whole change turns on): `decide_merge` is **already contradiction-aware** — `MergeOutcome` is `{Merge, KeepSeparate, Contradiction{clear_winner}}` and its prompt explicitly instructs the model to flag conflicting facts. Anything it leaves active is re-judged by Phase 2b regardless. And the *dangerous* path — deterministic dedup, the only route by which a fact leaves with no judgement and no record — is untouched and stays shut: **`contraBLIND` is 0 at every gate from 0.96 to 0.80**. This is also not a new mechanism; at 0.92 a reversed-argument pair already clustered (ADR-096). 0.89 raises exposure from 1 pair to 3, it does not create it.
> - **The classes are INTERLEAVED — no threshold can separate them.** Paraphrases sit at 0.8939–0.9025 (`design studio/agency` · `contacted by email` · `primary/mainly programs in Rust`) while knowledge-update contradictions sit at 0.9042–0.9049 (`standup 9am/10:30am` · `Berlin twice/four times` · `Vega/Atlas`). **Contradictions score ABOVE paraphrases here.** So "tune the number" can never be the whole answer, and the safety has to come from the judgement path — which is exactly where it comes from. Do not attempt to re-separate these by thresholding ([[feedback_fix_one_break_another_signals_structural]]).
> - **Kept honest across the codebase, not just at the one line:** `acceptance.rs` now derives its threshold from `ConsolidatorConfig::default()` (a hardcoded copy would have left the ship-gate grading a threshold the product no longer uses); `scale_eval.rs`'s `merge_gate_cos` likewise; the "contradictions sit below the gate so never cluster" claim was corrected in `phases/contradiction.rs`, `phases/mod.rs`, `phases/cluster.rs` and `consolidator.rs`'s Phase-2b comment, where it was stated as fact.
> - **✅ PROVEN LIVE — TWICE, BEFORE COMMIT (2026-07-27, founder-directed).** The one thing no sweep can model — *does Phi-4 merge a contradiction once clustering hands it one?* — was run for real against the 2.32 GB Phi-4 GGUF on fresh vaults, first at 0.89 and then at 0.84. Vectors verified present before each run (`divergence-check` N/N, no findings) — a memory with no vector is invisible to clustering and would have silently voided the test (ADR-095).
>
>   | | **0.89 run** (14 memories) | **0.84 run** (22 memories) |
>   |---|---|---|
>   | `cluster_count` | 6 | **10** |
>   | duplicate pairs merged | 3 | **4** |
>   | contradictions reaching `decide_merge` | 3 | **6** |
>   | **contradictions wrongly merged** | **0** ✅ | **0** ✅ |
>   | `contradictions_auto_resolved` | 3 | **6** |
>   | `clusters deduped` / `memories deduped` | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 |
>   | `clusters skipped` | 0 | 0 |
>   | controls (Mira / cello) | untouched | untouched |
>   | duration (debug build) | 4m29s | 11m13s |
>
>   **Phi-4 was handed six contradictions at 0.84 and declined to merge every one** — including `standup 9am`/`10:30am`, `MacBook Pro`/`ThinkPad`, `AWS`/`Azure`, `London`/`Manchester`. Phase 2b then retired the stale side of each by recency, keeping the current fact. The merged duplicate texts are clean and lossless: *"The user runs a small design studio/agency in London."* · *"The user prefers to be contacted by email."* · *"The user's primary programming language is Rust."* · *"The user prefers short, direct answers."* (that last one is the 10th pair, reachable only at 0.84). Every collapse is visible in the run summary — ADR-096's rendering fix doing its job.
>
>   **A chaining risk was deliberately baited and did not fire:** the 0.84 corpus contains *"The user lives in London."* alongside *"…design studio in London."* (cross-similarity 0.8213/0.8148, just under the gate). Ten clusters formed from ten real pairs with **no** cross-case chaining, and the unrelated control pair correctly did not cluster at all. **The revert trigger never fired; 0.84 ships.**
>
>   **Cost note, logged not hidden:** the 0.84 run took 11m13s for 22 memories vs 4m29s for 14 at 0.89 — more clusters means more Phi-4 calls. Debug build, offline nightly job, so not a concern now; it IS a scale input for the latency arc.
> - **Diagnostic is now a standing instrument, not a one-off.** `paraphrase_cluster_gate` gained Phase C (ship-gate precision/recall sweep) and Phase D (pinned test premises vs candidate gates). Re-run it — seconds, no rebuild — before touching the gate, the dedup axes, or any fixture whose route depends on where it sits relative to the gate.
> - **📌 Tech debt found in passing, NOT fixed here (no drive-by):** `co_topical_but_compatible_facts_are_not_falsely_invalidated` is **vacuous**. Its fixture (`"works ... at Atlas Structures"` / `"commutes to work by train"`) measures **0.6164** — below the 0.70 `CONTRADICTION_NN_SIMILARITY_FLOOR` — so the pair never becomes a candidate, its mock is never called, and the test passes because *nothing happens at all*. It has therefore never guarded against the false-positive it was written for. Pre-existing, unrelated to this change. Fix = re-pick the fixture into `[0.70, gate)` and add the same `call_count() >= 1` non-vacuity assertion the new below-gate test carries.

> **📐 ADR-098 (session 30) — an `ignore`-tagged doc fence is FORBIDDEN in `vault-llm`; illustrative snippets use `text`, pinned by a guard test. Fixes the weekly real-model smoke job, red since 2026-06-22.**
> - **Context:** `real-model smoke (weekly cron + run-llm-smoke label)` (`ci.yml:660`) failed **six consecutive weekly runs** — `30276542504` (07-27) · `29749777859` (07-20) · `29259145088` (07-13) · `28803916921` (07-06) · `28385029636` (06-29) · `27970037240` (06-22). It is our **only** automated check that the real Phi-4 GGUF still behaves, which ADR-097 made materially more important by staking a correctness decision on that model's judgement.
> - **Root cause — the tests were never the problem.** The three real-model tests **passed** (`3 passed`, 194s). The job then died on a **doctest**: `crates/vault-llm/src/phi4_mini.rs:67`, `error[E0433]` + `error[E0728]`, `Couldn't compile the test`, exit 1. The job runs `cargo test -p vault-llm -- --ignored --nocapture --test-threads=1` (`ci.yml:785`), and rustdoc registers an ` ```ignore ` fence as an `#[ignore]`d test — so **`--ignored` selects exactly the blocks marked "don't run" and forces them to compile.** The snippet is illustrative pseudo-code referencing `tauri_app`, a binding that does not exist, so it can never compile. The failure lands *after* a 194s model run, which is why it reads like a model regression and isn't one.
> - **Decision:** the fence becomes ` ```text `. Prose stays prose. Codified: in `vault-llm`, use `text` for anything that cannot compile, `no_run` for a real snippet that must compile but must not execute, `compile_fail` for one expected to fail — **never `ignore`.**
> - **VERIFIED BY REPRODUCTION, NOT BY READING** ([[feedback_source_read_call_graph_upstream_of_empirical]] — a handed-down diagnosis is a hypothesis until it is run). With the fence reverted, `cargo test -p vault-llm --doc -- --ignored` failed locally with a **byte-identical signature** to the GitHub log: same `E0425`/`E0433`/`E0728`, same `phi4_mini.rs - phi4_mini::Phi4MiniConfig (line 67)` test id, same `Couldn't compile the test`. With the fence fixed, the same command returns `running 0 tests … ok`. The GitHub log was also read in full to confirm the doctest is the **only** failing step, not merely the first.
> - **Why a one-word fix ships with a guard test.** This job has now been silently red for a month-plus **twice** — `ci.yml`'s own comment above the `--test-threads=1` flag records an earlier stretch ending 2026-05-18. A fix that any future doc edit can silently undo is not a fix. `crates/vault-llm/tests/doc_fences.rs` walks the crate's `src/` and fails on any `ignore`-tagged fence, naming `file:line` and explaining the CI consequence in the assertion message, so whoever trips it learns *why* rather than just *that*.
> - **The guard is negative-verified and non-vacuous** ([[feedback_flaky_bug_clean_run_proves_nothing]], [[feedback_privacy_invariants_need_dedicated_tests]]): it FAILED on the reverted fence, correctly pointing at `phi4_mini.rs:67`, and passes on the fix. It also asserts the directory walk found files at all — a guard whose scan silently returns nothing would pass forever, which is the same class of bug it exists to catch, and is exactly what made `co_topical_but_compatible_facts_are_not_falsely_invalidated` vacuous (ADR-097's tech-debt bullet).
> - **Cannot affect shipped behaviour.** The change is a documentation fence tag and a test-only file. No `src/` logic, no public API, no dependency change.
> - **Alternatives rejected:** (a) **delete the snippet** — it documents the one thing a caller genuinely needs (that resolving `model_dir` is the caller's job, per BRD §2.4); losing it to dodge a tag is a bad trade. (b) **make it a compiling `no_run` example** — would require `vault-llm` to depend on `tauri` purely to document a caller pattern, violating BRD §2's dependency flow. (c) **drop `--ignored` from the CI job** — that flag *is* the job; the real-model tests are `#[ignore]`d by design so they stay off every-PR runs. (d) **narrow the CI command to `--lib --tests` so doctests are skipped** — tempting and wrong: it would hide this class of failure rather than fix it, and would silently stop compiling doctests that *should* compile.
> - **🚨 THE ON-DEMAND TRIGGER NEVER EXISTED — found while trying to re-verify the fix.** `ci.yml`'s smoke job keys on `contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'run-llm-smoke')`, and **the `run-llm-smoke` label had never been created in this repo** (`gh label list` → the nine GitHub defaults plus `tech-debt` and `recurring-check`, nothing else). There was no `workflow_dispatch` either. So the job's condition was satisfiable **exactly one way: the Monday 12:00 UTC cron.** That is a large part of why six weeks of red went unnoticed — the only signal fired once a week, unattended, and the documented escape hatch silently matched nothing. **Following the session-29 instruction literally would have failed**, which is the ⚠️ "a warning about runtime behaviour is a hypothesis until it is checked" lesson landing on a CI config instead of a call graph.
>   - **Fixed:** `workflow_dispatch` added to `on:`, and `github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch'` added to the job's `if:` — so the alarm can now be asked a question at any time. A `workflow_dispatch` cannot fire by itself, so this widens nothing automatic. The `run-llm-smoke` label was also created, so the documented PR route works for the first time. **Generalisable lesson: a conditional trigger is not verified until it has actually fired once** — if a job's `if:` references a label, confirm the label exists.
> - **📌 Tech debt found in passing, NOT fixed here (no drive-by):** `vault-consolidator/src/consolidator.rs:5` carries the identical defect — a BRD §5.6 spec quote with `{ ... }` bodies under an ` ```ignore ` fence, which cannot compile. It is **dormant**: no CI job runs `--ignored` on that crate, so nothing forces it to compile today. Deliberately left because (i) different crate, (ii) touching it would pull vault-consolidator's ~35-minute, 12-binary, RAM-hungry test gate into a doc-fence commit. Fix = same one word, ideally alongside generalising `doc_fences.rs` into a workspace-wide guard.

> **⚠️ TWO TAURI GOTCHAS THAT COST TWO FAILED BUILDS — read before touching commands:**
> 1. **Adding a command is a TWO-file permission change.** `permissions/default.toml` DEFINES each `allow-*` (identifier + `commands.allow`); `capabilities/default.json` only REFERENCES it. Session 20's work order said "add `allow-*` permission in `capabilities/default.json`" — that instruction is INCOMPLETE and following it literally fails the build with *"Permission allow-X not found, expected one of …"*. Confirmed by reading `tauri-build-2.6.0/src/acl.rs`: with no explicit `AppManifest::commands` in `build.rs` (ours has none), Tauri falls back to reading the `permissions/` directory.
> 2. **`generate_handler!` cannot see through a `pub use`.** `#[tauri::command]` emits hidden companion items (`__cmd__<name>`, `__tauri_command_name_<name>`) beside the function; a re-export does not carry them. Register by DEFINING module path — `commands::memory::add_memory`, not `commands::add_memory`. Getting this wrong breaks EVERY command, including ones that previously worked.
>

Counts track deletes live (15→10), so they query rather than cache.

Vault lives at `%APPDATA%\com.shahbaz242630.memory-vault`. `vault-cli` reads the same OS keychain, so it needs no passphrase — but it is a SINGLE-WRITER vault: never run vault-cli while the app is open.

> **🧪 FRONTEND CONTRACT GUARDS ADDED (`crates/vault-tauri/tests/frontend_contract.rs`, 9 tests).** Pure `std` string analysis of the checked-in sources — no JS runtime, no DOM, no new dependencies, 0.01s. Closes the two gaps that actually bit us: (1) bans native `confirm(`/`prompt(` as action gates; (2) cross-checks the FOUR-file command wiring (`app.js` invoke → `#[tauri::command]` → `generate_handler!` → `permissions/default.toml` → `capabilities/default.json`). **Both of this session's build failures would have been caught by these**, in-crate and named. Includes a meta-test asserting the extractors find a non-empty, mutually-equal command surface — without it a parsing regression would make every guard pass vacuously.

> **▶️ THE ARC — packaging / installer (the last beta blocker; agreed with founder 2026-07-12, unchanged).**
> There is no outstanding UI work. The five pieces:
>   - **(a) Tauri bundler MSI** — the `bundle` block in `tauri.conf.json` half-exists but points at TEST-FIXTURE resources; real packaging must bundle or download the models instead.
>   - **(b) First-run model download** with progress UI — the models are too big to ship inside the MSI. Per ADR-086 the UI says "downloading recall engine", NEVER model names.
>   - **(c) `vault-cli` placement** — onboarding hands the user an MCP snippet saying `"command": "vault-cli"`, which is only TRUE if the installer puts `vault-cli` on PATH. The installer must make the snippet honest, or onboarding ships a lie.
>   - **(d) Code-signing decision — founder's call.** Unsigned MSI ⇒ Windows SmartScreen warnings on every install; signing costs money. Needs deciding, not engineering.
>   - **(e) ADR-038: the MSI launcher MUST set `LANCE_MEM_POOL_SIZE=268435456`** (WiX pre-args). Dev runs inherit it from `.cargo/config.toml`; **installed builds do NOT.** Confirmed live this session — launching the built exe directly required setting it by hand, which is exactly the installed-build condition. Miss this and installed vaults can OOM-abort on merge-heavy writes.
>
> **⚠️ Verify before building (a):** `tauri.conf.json`'s `bundle` block was last touched for slice 1's window/title changes — read it fresh, do not assume the fixture paths are the only staleness.
>
> **▶️ THE ARC AFTER PACKAGING — one-click agent connection (researched 2026-07-20 at founder's request; the last onboarding blocker for beta).**
> **The problem:** onboarding hands a non-coder a JSON block and says "put this in the right file". Four failure points — find the file, paste without breaking JSON, know to restart the agent, and get **no feedback at all** if it didn't work. Silent failure doesn't read as "I pasted it wrong", it reads as "this product is broken".
> **Research finding — why Hermes / OpenClaw have one-click and we cannot copy it.** Both are the **agent** (MCP client); their one-click is a curated catalog inside their own app. Hermes: entries are manifests merged into the `hermes-agent` repo under `optional-mcps/` — *"There is no community submission tier; entries are added by merging a PR"* (Nous reviews each). OpenClaw: same shape via ClawHub skills. **We are the SERVER.** Their button is not ours to build — but getting LISTED in their catalogs is a distribution/outreach action, not engineering.
> **The real lever for a server author: MCP Bundles (`.mcpb`)** — the open Desktop-Extensions format under `modelcontextprotocol/mcpb`. A zip of the server + `manifest.json`; the user **double-clicks one file and it installs**. No JSON editing, no config-file hunting, no terminal, no PATH dependency. Claude Desktop also has a browsable Anthropic-reviewed extensions directory (listing via an interest form).
> **⚠️ RETRACTION — supersedes my earlier same-session proposal.** I first proposed that the app WRITE the user's agent config files itself (locate `claude_desktop_config.json` etc., merge, atomic-write). **`.mcpb` is strictly better and that proposal is withdrawn:** config-writing risks corrupting another app's config, depends on `vault-cli` being on PATH (packaging item c), and creates an arbitrary-file-write security surface we would have to defend under §11. The bundle format removes all three, and is the vendor-supported path rather than reverse-engineering file locations that can change under us.
> **⚠️ OPEN QUESTION — fold into the PACKAGING spike, do not solve twice.** A bundle is meant to be self-contained, and the recall engine is >1 GB of model files. Whether a bundle can ship that, or must download on first run, is **the same question as packaging item (b)**. Do not assume it works — verify before designing around it.
> **Shape (once the size question is answered):** (1) `.mcpb` bundle = flagship one-click, biggest audience; (2) assisted config-write ONLY for agents with no bundle support (Cursor / Codex / Antigravity) — with backup + atomic write + fixed internal path allowlist, never a user- or agent-supplied path; (3) catalog listings (Anthropic directory, Hermes PR, ClawHub) = outreach, no code.
> **Ship a "Check connection" button alongside whichever route wins.** It answers the one question a non-coder cannot answer themselves — *did it actually work?* — and it helps even when the config was pasted by hand. Arguably higher value per unit of effort than the one-click write itself.
> **Sources:** anthropic.com/engineering/desktop-extensions · github.com/modelcontextprotocol/mcpb · claude.com/docs/connectors/building/mcpb · hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/user-guide/features/mcp · docs.openclaw.ai/cli/mcp
>

Definitively not a flake. **Diagnosis already exists** at `ci.yml:780-784`: the three smoke tests shared a single `.partial` download path so parallel runs raced, and `--test-threads=1` was added to fix it — it is STILL red, so that was not the whole story. Remaining suspects: the uncached 2.49 GB unsloth fetch every Monday (any HF 5xx or redirect-to-HTML fails the SHA gate) and VS2026/MSVC toolset churn. **Note the shape matches the `.partial` collision class fixed this session (ADR-087)** — worth checking whether the smoke tests still collide by another route. Only driving the REAL webview reproduces webview-specific behaviour.

> **🧹 Tech debt noted (not fixed — no drive-by):**
> 1. `CLAUDE.md` refers to the spec as `Agent Build Specification.txt` (spaces); the real filename is `Agent_Build_Specification.txt` (underscores). Costs a failed lookup at every session start.
> 2. **Version string is incoherent:** Settings renders `memory-vault 0.1.0 · V0.2 beta` — the `0.1.0` is real (`CARGO_PKG_VERSION`) but the crate has never been bumped, so the app reports 0.1.0 while the product is V0.2 beta. Honest but confusing to a user seeing both. Decide before beta: bump the crate to `0.2.0`, or drop the crate number from the UI.
> 2b. **✅ RESOLVED session 22 — reranker had no acquisition path.** Now downloads on demand (ADR-087). Its provenance is still absent from `MODEL_PROVENANCE.md`, which documents only bge/tokenizer/ORT — the pinned hashes and source repo live in `vault_embedding::integrity` + `model_fetch`. Worth reconciling so there is one provenance story, not two.
> 2c. **PARTLY RESOLVED session 23 — progress landed; RESUME did not.** `ensure_model_at_path_with_progress` now reports; `ensure_model_at_path` is still restart-not-resume by design (ADR-043, chosen when Phi-4 was the only caller and nothing blocked on it). A drop at 90% still means starting over, and that now happens under an onboarding gate. The progress seam and the resume seam turned out NOT to be the same code — progress is a callback in the chunk loop, resume needs a Range header plus a rehash of the existing `.partial` — so the earlier "do them together" note was wrong and resume is now standalone work. Revisit if beta users on slow links report it; do not pre-build it.
> 2d. **NEW — `verify_file_sha256` reads the WHOLE file into RAM.** `vault-embedding/src/integrity.rs:68` does `std::fs::read(path)?` then hashes the buffer, so verifying the 1.15 GB reranker allocates 1.15 GB. ADR-088 fixed the *other* hashing path (`vault-llm`'s `compute_sha256_of_file`, now 8 MB chunks on `spawn_blocking`) but this second implementation was missed — and it is the one `Qwen3RerankerProvider::open` calls on every first-use load. On a machine with ~6 GB free (see [[project_build_disk_thrash_and_link1318]]) that spike is real. Fix is mechanical: reuse the chunked helper. Not done here — no drive-by, and it is not in this task's blast radius.
> 3. **No operator visibility into boundaries or the audit log.** `vault-cli` has no `boundary list` and no `audit verify|tail`; the only way to inspect either is the GUI. For a product whose pitch is a tamper-evident audit trail, that is a real gap — it blocks supporting a beta user's vault and blocks the founder inspecting their own without the UI. Surfaced 2026-07-20 when CLI-verifying a UI-created boundary proved impossible.
> 4. **No frontend BEHAVIOUR coverage.** The contract guards above are source-level only; nothing exercises a click. This is what the E2E arc addresses.

## 2 · 🧭 Where the build is

V0.2 read/consolidate core is functionally complete and CI-green. The work since T0.2.3 was a long correctness-at-the-output arc (the founder thesis: *"memory is only useful if the output is correct"*). Net result:

- **Read path** returns structured facts, NO LLM at read (`StructuredReadPipeline`, ~500ms). The calling agent composes the answer. Recall-first by lock: never false-empty.
- **`memory_read`** is the primary answer path (returns structured `abstain`); **`memory_search`** is reorder-only + recall-safe (never false-empties) with an additive `weak_match` hint. (ADR-066/069/071)
- **Reranker** (Qwen3-Reranker-0.6B, cross-encoder) is the read relevance authority, lazily loaded off the MCP handshake. (ADR-059/070)
- **Consolidator** produces a per-boundary REPORT (structured knowledge state) nightly; contradiction detection is nearest-neighbor based. (ADR-053/065)
- **Cross-agent proven:** Claude, Cursor, Antigravity all read the vault correctly. Validated at 100 facts live across both tools and both model tiers.
- **Scale:** `scale_eval` harness shows correctness is scale-invariant 100→1k→10k (identical scorecard). The one 10k internal crash (a flaky, data-safe storage-worker race) is fixed + shipped (ADR-072).

**Last shipped commit:** `da10c0f` (ADR-072, 10k TOCTOU fix), CI-green run `27096332980`. Recent chain: `a3c938b`→`661d391`→`a1e4dac`→`da10c0f` all matrix-clean.

**The locked arc** ([[locked-next-arc-t03x]], amended 2026-05-26) — all four steps SHIPPED:
1. ✅ MCP `memory.write` description hardening (`93d1410`)
2. ✅ Consolidator → REPORT (Batch A, `f0cc158`, ADR-053)
3. ✅ Read returns structured facts, no LLM at read (Batch B Commit 6, `99052f2`, ADR-052/054)
4. ✅ Consolidator wired into runtime + manual CLI trigger (`f0cc158`)

Phase C (write-time decision loop) DEFERRED to V1.0+.

## 3 · 🔒 Architectural locks (do not relitigate without explicit founder sign-off)

- **LLM is OUT of the read path** (2026-05-26). The read consumer is itself an LLM (the agent); pre-composing prose was redundant. Vault returns structured facts; agent composes. Delivered ~170× local speedup, ~50× BYOK cost cut, ~10× Managed PAYG margin. Phi-4-mini stays at nightly consolidation only. [[project_architectural_lock_llm_out_of_read_path]]
- **Recall is sacrosanct.** A false-abstain (vault has the answer but says "I don't know") is the cardinal sin — far worse than a false-answer. Every read/search change is recall-safe by construction: reorder-only, never false-empty. [[project_memory_read_primary_search_recall_safe]]
- **Correctness of output IS the product.** Storage + retrieval are table stakes; correct output to the agent is the differentiator. Don't burn cycles on prose polish when the structured field is already correct. [[project_correctness_is_the_product]] · [[feedback_structured_contract_user_sees_via_agent]]
- **Correctness before latency** (V0.2). Get core quality to 100% first; don't preempt latency work until the founder signals the core is structurally solid. [[project_correctness_before_latency]]
- **Three-mode deployment** (Local $10 / BYOK $5mo / Managed PAYG) shares one codebase; every architectural decision must be mode-agnostic. Managed = per-user vault + per-user key. [[project_three_mode_deployment]] · [[project_managed_mode_per_user_vault]]
- **Zero-knowledge guarantee:** the server cryptographically cannot read vault contents. No crypto-path change without re-reading BRD §11 + an ADR-SEC entry.
- **Never recommend sub-7B models for read-time synthesis** (Qwen2.5-7B is the quality floor) — moot now that the LLM is out of read, but stands if read-synthesis is ever revisited. [[feedback_no_sub_7b_models_for_synthesis]]
